ParlaMint-DK_20170517143425	Can the government confirm that there is an agreement to complete the Holstebro motorway in case there is a retreat?
ParlaMint-DK_20170517143436	Yes, please. And we ask the Minister to answer.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517143441	That was close to the printed question, I would say to Mr. Andreas Steenberg, so here's the answer. Thank you for the question. The establishment of a motorway between Herning and Holstebro is in full swing, and the first stage at Herning opens at the end of May. As you know, the solution under way is the result of a broad settlement between the Left, the Liberal Alliance, the Conservative People's Party, the Social Democratic Party, the Danish People's Party, the Radical Left and the Socialist People's Party from 2013. At that time, efforts were made to obtain funding for as much motorway as possible. In fact, it was exceptionally decided to borrow 452 million. kr. of the general public investment framework for this motorway project. The 2013 agreement states that these funds are to be returned to the general public investment framework by a possible decrease in consumption in the overall construction project. If it turns out that any cost-cutting of the project is greater than this amount, the remaining amount will be returned to the Infrastructure Fund. Unfortunately, however, we are currently in a situation where there is very little room for new large-scale infrastructure investments unless more funds are provided. Therefore, the government is working in the context of the political negotiations to resolve the challenges up to 2025 to find a basis for increasing the framework for public investment. Once funds are available again, the last kilometres of motorway around Herning will be included on an equal footing with other infrastructure projects in the political priority of the funds.

The majority of the aid for accessibility is granted for the construction of lifts in connection with the conversion of general housing units and for improved accessibility in the associated dwellings. It is the individual housing organisations that apply for support with an explanation of the improvements in accessibility that the housing organisation wishes to set in motion. The application must be approved by the municipal council. The commitment to support from the Landsbygningfonden is assessed inter alia. in relation to the supply of available housing in the area and the design of the buildings. There is therefore a thorough preparatory work on the applications received by the Landsbygningsfonden from the housing organisations. In other words, the allocation of funds is based on applications from housing organisations. Housing organisations are close to their users and can therefore assess the need for improvements in accessibility and needs in the specific housing department. Against this background, I believe that the process of allocating funds is appropriate.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517154322	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Health. And she's very welcome up here.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517154353	Thank you, sir. President. The motion for a resolution calls on the government to change the rules of practice so that blood donation is also possible for men having sex with men. In the following I would like to call for ease the group msm, which I also know is used as a term in many of these contexts. The proposers want msm to be able to donate blood in the Danish blood banks on an equal footing with everyone else. At the same time, it is proposed to make changes to the questions to which donors must respond before tapping. The questions must be the same for both sexes, and men must not be asked if they have had sex with another man. B 118 is justified by the fact that current practice contributes to unfair discrimination and stigmatisation of msm. The authors do not believe that this is technically justified in practice and that any virus in the blood can be detected by the current screening methods. It is the responsibility of the government and our responsibility as health politicians to ensure that donated blood has high quality and the minimum risk to the sick who are to receive the blood. And we must also keep in mind that blood only needs to be bottled to help our sick fellow citizens. Therefore, individuals cannot be donors if they have infection that can be transmitted with their blood. A lot of people are permanently excluded from donating blood. This is true, for example. all with cancer, infectious diseases and diseases of central organs such as stomach, intestines, kidneys and lungs etc. People with risky sexual behavior also belong to that group because their behavior statistically increases the risk of developing diseases that can be transmitted with their blood. There are also very many who are excluded from blood donation for shorter or longer periods of time. They can, for example. become if they have had infections, use certain medications, have been travelling in an area of malaria or have another high risk of infection. However, there are no rules without exceptions, and while in Denmark we exclude msm from blood donation, there is in principle the possibility of derogating from it after a concrete assessment in the blood bank. The selection of donors is of the utmost importance for the quality of blood and therefore also for patient safety in Denmark. The Government agrees with the proposers that there may be a need to carry out donor selection in the best possible way. I think we all have a wish for this, and that is why the government is also prepared to carry out a review of the blood banks' process of assessing the suitability of the donor. We, like the proposers, want donors to be asked as carefully as possible about their health and other aspects of blood quality. At the same time, however, the issues must also be sufficiently comprehensive to ensure that all relevant information is provided for the assessment of the blood banks. Today, Msm is generally excluded from blood donation on the basis of the Board of Patient Safety's professional risk assessment, and, like previous governments on both the blue and the red sides, the current government considers it best to follow the board's professional assessment. In other words, we want to maintain current practice and thus reject B 118. I would like to point out that that attitude is in no way about discrimination against homosexuality, which I can hear some people want to do. Women who have sex with other women are not excluded because of their sexual behavior. They are assessed as other donors. The government and the attitude of previous governments are based on the fact that, in relation to msm and blood treatment alone, it is a matter of professional safety, in other words, that in patient treatment there is a consideration of the seriously ill who need and need blood examination and treatment. I think we all know that in the 1980s there was an unhappy hemorrhage at home, where around 90 bleeding patients received HIV from infected blood products. Since then, the quality and safety of blood transfusions and blood products have been the highest priority in Denmark. A general exclusion of msm is based on three factual facts, as reported by the Patient Safety Agency. Firstly, there is an increased risk of infection. Msm is estimated to have fifty-fold higher rates of HIV and an increased incidence of hepatitis B compared to heterosexuals. And in recent years, the number of new HIV cases in msm has increased by 33 per cent. in the EU and in the EEA countries. There has been an increase of 83% in the 15-19 age group, with an increase of 50 per cent. with msm between 20 and 24 years of age. Secondly, some msm has HIV, which has not yet been diagnosed. Therefore, they are not in a treatment that prevents transmission of HIV via blood, and they can therefore infect others. Thirdly, it can be infected with, among other things. HIV is not recorded in the screening of bottled blood if the infection has occurred shortly before. And that is despite the fact that the proposers claim that infection can also be detected during the so-called window phase. Therefore, transmission of infection cannot be completely excluded even with the current effective test methods. I would like to emphasise that the government's position is in line with the current EU regulation in this area. Since 2002, uniform and high requirements for the quality and safety of blood have been introduced throughout the Union. Quality levels have been raised at all stages. This is not only in relation to blood collection and testing requirements, but also in relation to quality management and control of blood banks. The most important element in the use of donor blood is, of course, the selection of donors themselves. Therefore, Donor's suitability must be thoroughly examined before each tap can be clarified whether a person should be permanently excluded, be in temporary quarantine, or there may be other problems. One of the firm criteria in that assessment is the sexual behaviour of potential donors. In Denmark, as I said, this criterion has led to the general exclusion of men having sex with men. This criterion can in principle be waived following a specific assessment in the blood bank. That is also what I said earlier, and I think that is, in fact, essential to some of the points raised by the authors in their proposals. The government is aware that some EU countries have relaxed their demands, allowing blood donation from men who have not had sex with another man in the past 12 months. In Sweden, msm has a 12-month Karen period of blood donation, and in Sweden, msm is also excluded from donating plasma, i.e. plasma to drug production. The Swedish authorities expect to revise their rules in the field of blood, and in this context they will assess and take a position on their practice for msm. Sweden's review in this area is expected to be completed by the end of 2018, and it will of course be interesting to look at also in a Danish context. As Minister for Health, I fully acknowledge that there are men who have sex with men and really want to give blood for the benefit of their fellow human beings. I have great respect for and am grateful for the around 230,000 Danes who voluntarily and without payment actively contribute to donating blood and blood plasma. They provide a great deal of social effort to ensure that the Danish health service has sufficient and stable self-sufficiency in blood. Unfortunately, however, as I said, it is not the case that everyone can be donors. There are a large number of groups that are excluded from being blood donors in Denmark, and as long as the health assessment advocates the exclusion of msm, the government would rather wear belts and braces. And today, in the rules, there are the possibilities here that, on the basis of a concrete assessment, you can be accepted as a donor. Because we will not compromise on security. It is ultimately the safety of patients that we must put at the top when it comes to blood donation in Denmark.

Today is the international day against homophobia, and I can hardly imagine a better opportunity for us to take the opportunity here at Christiansborg to make it clear that no matter who you love, no matter what sexual orientation you have, everyone must have the same fundamental rights. It can't change love or sexual preferences, and that's how it's supposed to be. Donating blood is not a right, but it is an opportunity that 230,000 people in Denmark voluntarily use to help another person in need. And from here, a great thank you is due to Danish blood donors. If we on the Left do not support the resolution, then it is not because we believe that men who have sex with men should not have the same rights as others in our society. In the left, we're preoccupied with patient safety. Statistically, men who have sex with men have approx. The incidence of HIV is approximately 50-fold higher and the incidence of hepatitis B is increased compared to heterosexuals. Especially in view of the so-called window phase of up to 2 weeks, where you can be infected with HIV without knowing it, do we not think that this is a good starting point for changing the existing rules? Does this mean that this should be so forever? No, screening and treatment techniques can be improved in the future and there can be other ways than general exclusion; in the Left we are open to discussion of the possibilities. The Left cannot support the motion for a resolution, but we would like to contribute to a report in this area, in which we would like to stress that it is not a question of equality, but of patient safety, of security for the recipient, just as we would like to see a review of the blood banks' process in relation to the assessment of each donor, so that, for example, we can do something about it. ensure that the questionnaires provided ask both accurate and non-stigmatizing. I will certainly take part in such a hearing with pleasure and interest.

For us, state governance is precisely about the same thing: supporting the best possible development of health care and ensuring the highest possible health for the total resources that we have for our health care services, all for the benefit of the patient. It is money that can be released to other places in our healthcare system. The government is already focusing on a change of track in the control. The focus so far has largely been on activity, but in 2016 we launched the national quality programme, which ensures a more balanced management of both activity, quality and value for the patient. The programme consists of several important initiatives in which the government, inter alia: Together with KL and Danske Regions, eight national quality objectives have been agreed in the field of health. We have also launched a national management programme to develop local management, including: strengthen the skills of managers for the ambitious quality and improvement work to be done. For there is no doubt that good governance is also necessary if we are to have a strong and effective healthcare system. This is crucial, because governance is primarily about management. It's about how we're looking. It is not a question of detailed regulation, and it is not a question, as the debate is often a little caricatured, of saving 2%. in all departments. It is all about constantly looking at where we can be more efficient and where we may need to spend more resources in our health area, and precisely to ensure that we develop it. The government has also extended the right of investigation and treatment, so patients have a greater assurance not to be on a waiting list. And it is, in fact, also a crucial increase in the quality of the patient. When we talk about quality in health care, it is not just about what we are talking about here as governance and about 2%, as Enhedslisten does in their resolution. It is also about the quality of the patient. And there is no doubt that it means something to the patient that you are not on the waiting list but can get quickly into treatment. We also thank and thank you for the support for this phased out the Danish quality model for accreditation of the public hospitals. It is also all to ensure that we use our powers and resources to create quality in healthcare and not bureaucracy. Finally, the government has supported the regions in their work with a more value-based management, which will help ensure more value for the patient in the specific treatment, and there are currently a number of trials running around the regions, where there is a focus on quality for the individual patient. So no, the government does not turn its back on the management of the Danish healthcare system. We need smart and intelligent management that supports the development of the health sector, a development that must address the challenges that are also in the healthcare sector, where we face, among others, the challenges we face. more elderly citizens, some of whom have a need for treatment. Understand me correctly, as I said, the whole discussion about governance is not about the nurse going 102 steps instead of 100. It is about developing health care for the benefit of both the patient and the employees as well as the relatives. It is precisely a matter of ensuring that the nurse can only take 95 steps instead of the 100 steps, because one is constantly looking at where new technology can be introduced, where workflows can be made more efficient, where the new quality fund buildings can help to make it more flexible in the hospital. So this isn't really about blind green harvesting of our health care. The health service has also proved that they can work both better and smarter. Over the past 10 years, productivity has increased by 2.4 per cent. I think it is important to bear this in mind when we are discussing what Parliament or the Enhedslisten want to impose on us in an economic agreement with the regions. We must constantly focus on what creates quality for the patient, how we get more health for the money, and there is no doubt that in many areas today patients are faster and also more gentle than they have been in the past, among others. thanks to new technology and a adaptable healthcare system, where employees are also constantly helping to develop our health services, where what works somewhere is being widely used to work in many other departments as well. If you just take a concrete example, it could be the robotic technology that helps assist surgical procedures, the so-called da Vinci robots, which we have now got 20-25 of here at home. They are now helping to implement around 80%. of the prostate surgery, and the robot is, as it were, the surgeon's extended arm. It has ensured that the operation has become more gentle, and thus getting technology in has also, if we look at a large number of other treatment areas, helped to ensure shorter admissions, helped to ensure that the treatments are less costly. Is this form of efficiency and control bad for patients? No, it's not. It helps to free up resources that we can use elsewhere in our healthcare services for the benefit of patients and for the benefit of healthcare workers. It is important that our healthcare system constantly improves and delivers a better quality and value to the patient. Having said that, we must of course also develop and improve governance. We must not only develop health care. We also need to look at governance. We need to think about whether the productivity requirement is always the best way to make up for the gains, and that is also what the government has done with the control review we have announced, which is also the broad adoption that was adopted here in Parliament and thanks to the parties that supported it: what works and what does not work in the goal of ensuring the development of health care for the benefit of patients and employees. However, it is also important that we not only believe that from one day to the next we can throw our health care from the governance we have now to no governance. We need to do and ensure a thorough work, and that is what the control review should be so that we do not jump to conclusions or we will not fall into such light headlines without really knowing what works and what can help improve and develop our health care. Our health services are a complex area, and therefore it is also important that we are thorough, that the ideas that suddenly come out and meet reality in the departments are also well thought out, that they are also tested in such a way that they do not risk being destructive rather than beneficial. Therefore, we need to be careful just to think that from one day to the next we can change the way we have managed our health care. We have to listen to those who have the daily walk in the hospitals. This is also what both the Minister for Public Innovation and I will do in the context of the governance review. We will meet them, we will listen to those who have contact with health care every day, be it doctors, nurses, patients and relatives. It is important to get their view on it, and it is also important that they speak up in relation to what they think is working and what does not work, and it is important that we take it into account in the further work. That is also what was included in the joint adoption on top of the question debate, because we need to focus on continuing to reap benefits and develop our health care. We need to focus on how we constantly improve the framework for our common health for the benefit of patients and employees. Then there are some who think that a resolution should be adopted requiring it to be abolished right now. There, I think, we should do something else carefully, particularly in respect of the staff of our health services, particularly in respect of those patients who are entitled to health care treatment all the time, that it is solid, that it is based on the fact that we are actually doing what we know is working and not because we are thinking that things must now be done quickly for the sake of speed. It is crucial for the government that we support the development of health services for the benefit of patients and relatives and employees, and that is also what we are already doing both with the national quality programme and with the eight national objectives and with the control review, and that is also the work that we will continue both in the ongoing negotiations with the regions and in the work that the health rapporteurs and the minister are doing in this Chamber, and that is also what we are constantly working for, both in the context of the financial agreement here for 2018 and in the context of the governance review, namely to improve our health services. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution tabled by the Enhedslisten.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517173044	The requirement for productivity, yes, there has certainly been a lot of spin on that story following the debate that took place last week. Last week, in the Danish People's Party, we tried to make a decision with the SF in connection with the question, but unfortunately, when you make a proposal for adoption, if there is no majority in favour of it, it will not be put to the vote, and it did not last week. Hey! So we agreed to that, but it was quick that we should have voted no. This is probably one of the spins that I like the least in Danish politics, so that we do not try to explain to the public what it is about, rather than spin on it. We think the same thing today as we thought last week, and that is that we want the productivity requirement gone, and that is why we agree with the resolution. Because we think it's time to get rid of it. There have been very good rapporteurs up here who have said that the time for the requirement of productivity is coming to an end, and there have also been some who have said on some issues that it had not worked. But it has worked for so many years. In the years I have been on the Health Committee and have been involved in many of the decisions that have been taken to get away waiting lists, to create patient rights, to create rights for the individual patient, has surely made us have got hold of waiting lists and that we have managed many of the things that are needed in hospitals. But we can also see that perhaps we have now reached where we have got so many different measuring instruments, so many control instruments, that this exact requirement puts increased pressure on some things that make it time to say that there may be plenty of steering instruments in advance. This productivity requirement, together with the additional activity benefit, also means that more and more needs to be done. We are very concerned in the Danish People's Party when we hear about medical patients on the hallways. We cannot get them out more quickly unless we ask the municipalities to take care of them, as we see being done, but we should not do so until they can take care of them. We have also heard this with the women who are giving birth, but we cannot send them out more quickly either, and we have had many discussions over the past few years about how busy there has been in the maternity wards. So the question is, where is it that we can do an additional activity in our hospitals? It's not all departments, it's just some of them. In the regions, they have a budgetary ceiling, which is a guarantee of treatment, and one of the most recent ones is the quality targets, which are precisely going in and measuring parameters to say that it is the quality that needs to be measured. There is no point in being quick to operate a knee if there is not a rehabilitation or a municipality that takes over afterwards. I was out in Region Sjælland the other day, and now I am not in the habit of commending the regions, but here I actually thought there were some very good initiatives. I know that in Region Sjælland and in some of the other regions you have created this value-based management, not looking at the extra activity, but that it should have a value for the individual patient, so that you follow the quality, not just when you treat, but all the way through to the municipality, and that is also what you control. They say out there that it does not mean that you do not make it more efficient because more activity is being taken away. You know that you look at quality instead, and then you can easily end up having streamlined what you did in the past in a different way. ParlaMint-DK_20170517174212	Then we have again, one is tempted to say. The Enhedslisten has tabled a motion for a resolution on the abolition of productivity requirements in the regions. The productivity requirement is a tool that has proved to be both effective and useful to achieve the objective. The challenge is surely basically that we currently lack an overview of which control tools we could possibly replace the productivity requirement and with what effect. Let's be honest for a second. Sometimes the productivity requirement is presented as if the employees were to run 2%. faster, or that the birthers must give birth to 2%. In the field of health and safety at work, there is a need for a more rapid response to the demand for productivity in psychiatry. And that doesn't really hold out. And let me give you an example. Looking operations for the treatment of intestinal cancer over previous conventional operations have reduced hospitalisation days from 12 days to 6 days. An operation now costs DKK 70,000, where it used to cost $100,000. Does the patient experience a deterioration? No. The surgical technique is better for the patient, who even gets home more quickly in the familiar surroundings. When regions reorganise workflows, buy in cheaper and make more efficient use of new medicines and new technologies and hospital buildings, it is, of course, an expression of the most health for money, and it makes sense to think in particular that the efficiency gains that the regions reap will remain in the regions and that the funds can be used for new initiatives or initiatives where they make the most sense. If I am to return to the Einstein quotes, it should be clear to everyone that as a society we have an obligation to try to see things from new angles and look at new ways of doing things. And we must constantly ask ourselves: Can we do things wiserly? From this point of view, a control review has now been initiated. We need to find wise answers to what works and what doesn't work. What do we need less of, and what do we need more of? Let me conclude by congratulating the regions on the fact that, if we look at the average over the last 10 years, the regions have actually delivered more than the 2% agreed. And that reinforces my belief that there is Yet we in the Left alone chose last year to raise health care by 2.4 billion. kr. That priority is an indication that we in the Left give priority to the health of the Danes, just as we have a strong desire to have the most health for the money. The Left cannot support the motion for a resolution, but we are happy to contribute to a report based on the need for a health service characterised by balance, with a focus on activity, quality and patient safety. And from the Left side we are open to looking at new control options.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517174857	The most important thing in the Danish healthcare system is that patients receive good treatment and that employees have good conditions and a meaningful working day. We hear so many times that nurses can't run any faster. No, and neither are they. I don't want nurses running any faster. They shouldn't run at all, they should go and they should go the right way, but that requires good leadership, which can clearly tell which way to go and which takes the lead. And then we have to trust the civil servants. I believe that people take an education and a job because they want to use the acquired skills to perform their jobs as best they can. It is not okay when nurses stress around for a whole day, work like horses and yet go frustrated home because they do not feel that they have had the opportunity to do their job well enough to all patients. But the cause of this stress is not the requirement for productivity. It's the settlement system and the bureaucracy. We'll take the bureaucracy first. Danish Nursing Council has calculated itself that they spend so much time on unnecessary bureaucracy that it corresponds to approx. 3,000 full-time positions. If we get rid of that part of the documentation which is superfluous, nurses can therefore have so much more time available that it is equivalent to employing 3,000 new nurses. If the same applies to other staff groups, much more time can be made available to patients without employing more staff. This government has set in motion work to realise as much of this efficiency potential as possible, so I hope and believe that it can finally become more bureaucratic than just talk. Then to the settlement system. The fault with the settlement system is that it only rewards actions, i.e. quantity and not quality. Instead, we have to go where we reward regions, hospitals, management and other staff for actions that give more value to patients and not just unconsciously reward actions, whether they should have been done or not. An example could be knee surgery. We have seen examples of knee surgery being carried out, despite the fact that it would be cheaper for the system and better for the patient to get rehabilitation. When the operation is performed anyway, it is because the hospital is rewarded better for the operation than for the rehabilitation. That is why we need a settlement system that rewards actions that give more value to the patient, rather than just looking at the number of, for example. operations. We need a settlement system that combines quantity with quality and value, and this can be done without abolishing the productivity requirement. The requirement of productivity does not harm patients or employees; however, the settlement system does. If we want to be able to provide the best treatment for all patients, it is important to maintain the productivity requirement. We must have as much value as possible for the money, and remember that this should not mean that the staff should run stronger on the contrary. Fortunately, we are well on the road through the eight national targets and some 20 key indicators, just as a national management programme has been set in motion and a control review has also been started. In addition, I would like to point out that the productivity requirement is not a green harvester, as has been repeatedly called it. It is precisely designed to give skilled managers the freedom to practise it in those departments where technological development allows for greater efficiency than elsewhere. It's populist bullshit when politicians say women can't give birth to 2%. Faster. Of course, they cannot and must not do so, and there is absolutely no point in the requirement of productivity. Finally, it should be noted that it is also possible to learn from private companies which can carry out many operations and other actions, such as: scans, much cheaper than government. Some savings could also be made by allowing private hospitals to carry out much more of the tasks they do best. At the same time, it would give the public better space to take care of the tasks they are best at. Finally, it should be noted that the resources released as a result of the productivity requirement remain in the regions, so that they can be used precisely for the benefit of patients and employees. Only 2% has been agreed. and not 2.4%, which the regions have delivered on average over the last 10 years. The Liberal Alliance is against the proposal, and I should like to welcome the Conservatives and say that they are also against it. However, we are happy to support a report which provides for a better settlement, which was also read out by the Social Democrat rapporteur, and I welcome that from the Conservatives and say that they too do. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517181051	Could the rapporteur just give us two, three, four examples of what we will achieve the most health for money?
ParlaMint-DK_20170517181058	The rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517181059	In fact, I have done so in my rapporteur's speech, where I have just highlighted diagnostic centres as a very good example of the fact that the regions have chosen to call patients for examinations in one day rather than to call patients for treatment in several days, although it is in fact the several days of treatment that would have given the regions the much-needed economy. Instead, however, it has been decided to do the job in a different way, because it is most appropriate for the patient and it is also more rational for the staff in the end. This, on the other hand, gives the regions a poorer economy, but it is a very good example of how the regions themselves have some good ideas on how to solve the tasks more appropriately without using the 2-percent requirement.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517181142	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no more comments, and then it is the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Mrs Stine Brix, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517181151	Thank you for the debate. This is a debate which, in my view, can easily be repeated here in the Chamber, because from my chair it is one of the most important in terms of health care right now. Our proposal is that the government should work to abolish the requirement for the productivity of hospitals in the agreement currently being negotiated with Danske Regions on the economy of the regions. The requirement of productivity means that every year hospitals have to deliver 2%. more activity, without money accompanying it. It is a requirement that has existed in various forms since around the year 2000 and, at least in the last few years, has sounded at 2%. It may not sound so much that you have to deliver 2%. more, but the damage happens when you leave a claim for so many years, then it becomes a lot of pressure on the hospitals and on the staff. The effect of the demand over the last ten years has therefore been that the same staff as we had ten years ago must today raise 22 per cent. More tasks. This has led to a worse working environment in hospitals, where both doctors, nurses, SOSU assistants and cleaning staff report that they are stressed and under time pressure and as a result make mistakes. This also endangers the patient's safety. As an example of this we saw, the Danish Labour Inspectorate in 2014 distributed 37 orders for bad working conditions with direct reference to time pressure and stress. All groups working in the health sector agree that the requirement for productivity is the problem and they urge it to be abolished. Danish Regions, which own and operate the hospitals, agree across all parties that the requirement of productivity must be abolished. Danish patients, who bring together a large number of our patient organisations, believe that it is time for a fight with the productivity requirement. In fact, the government and the Social Democrats here in the Folketing are gradually the only ones left with support for the productivity requirement. We hear from the government parties that it is about giving more health for the money. Most recently, we heard it from the rapporteur of the Left who asked questions to my colleague from the SF just now. The fact is, however, that we can see that the requirement of productivity leads to less health for the money. This encourages as much activity as possible without any consideration being given to the appropriateness of this activity for patients. We have seen examples from Silkeborg and Vejle which tell us that, despite the fact that the current regime is actually punishing them, they are still trying to arrange the whole thing in an appropriate way for patients. But that is in spite of the productivity requirement. We have also heard in the past that one could simply not have so many unintended incidents. If some of the many unintended incidents were eliminated, productivity could also be increased, as the Minister said back in February. But then we have not listened at all to what the staff are saying, because it is precisely because of the urgency that the incidents occur. When the pace of work is pushed up too high, yes, more mistakes are made, and that is also to the detriment of getting a good health service. The Minister also said back in February that we should also abolish re-hospitals. I fully agree with that, but that is precisely where we are at the core of the productivity requirement. For one way of increasing productivity is of course by having very fast printing, and also too fast printing, and then we see rehousing. So we have a productivity requirement that works directly opposite to what I actually think we all think is important, namely that we get as much health as possible for the money. Several rapporteurs from the government parties then say that technology can increase productivity. It is also true that it can technology sometimes, and it can new medicine sometimes. And sometimes it can't. Then it costs money, but fortunately it gives patients more health. This story of the fact that it is only the productivity requirement that makes us have new technology in health care, I think is simply too far-fetched. So, is it really the view that the health service would not have acquired new technologies if there had not been a productivity requirement? Of course you would have acquired it. Of course, we would have had robotic surgery, even if we had not had a productivity requirement. We are, of course, following the developments that are taking place, fortunately, because it can be for the benefit of the patient, both when it brings productivity, but also when it costs, because it is about having a good health service. It is not surprising to me that the government, which at the time, at least with some of the same parties, was in favour of the famous efficiency plan in the SKAT, believes that we can continue and continue to make it more efficient. It was also the same line of thought that led to the launch of a major efficiency plan for SKAT. But I am more disappointed by the Socialists, who, after all, have come to the realization that the requirement of productivity is harmful. After all, the Socialists have the opportunity to send out a very clear signal from the majority of the Danish Parliament to the government that it is now necessary to work to abolish the productivity requirement. That signal cannot be sent by a majority because the Socialists will not support the proposal. The Socialists' excuse is that you cannot abolish the productivity requirement until a new management system has been found. As I also said to the Socialist rapporteur, I do not think that makes sense. I think it is equivalent to finding that the improvements in the efficiency of the SKAT over many, many years were harmful, and then to conclude that it should just keep going until it has been found out what is going to happen. The logical thing would be to stop and say: Now we should stop doing more of what is damaging to the sector, just as the Radical Left has proposed in their proposal, that is to say, using the economic agreement in 2018 to pause it. And then, if a majority in the Folketing wants to introduce new governance models, which are, moreover, very unclear, what is the point, then we can introduce them afterwards. Then we'll reduce the damage and stop the accident. If both the Socialists and the Minister are guilty of the strawman, that there is no control if the productivity requirement is abolished, then I have to say that that is not true. Paradoxically, the Minister himself mentions several of the management tools we have in the law for patients' rights, national health objectives and, of course, it is quite obvious to combine the things we already have with two simple requirements, namely respect for the budgets of the regions, when an agreement is made to transfer block grants to them and, moreover, to provide a block grant corresponding to the tasks that you would like to have solved. If you want more activity, which you have been given free of charge for the past many years by demanding increased productivity, then you have to pay for it. I think that the essence of this discussion is whether you trust the regions to manage hospitals responsibly. There is nothing to prevent, for example, the possibility of increasing productivity in a single department. To introduce new technology, it can be done even if there is no centralized productivity requirement. You can do that, and you have to do that. However, with this proposal, we are transferring responsibility to the regions which are operating on a day-to-day basis, i.e. the responsibility to organise when productivity is increased and how it is done. We do not have a strict requirement to achieve a target of 2%. of the total somatic health care budget. And I actually thought that was what the Socialists' so-called 'away' reform was about: trust in those who work with the area and trust that the regionalists who sit out there can manage the budgets and comply with them and ensure that we have as much health as possible for the money, in other words that we have a real opportunity to assess where it makes sense to increase productivity and where it does not. Because with the system we have today it is so that if you decide to say that you exempt the maternity wards, even though we in here from Christiansborg have counted it into what to increase productivity for, yes, you just have to impose another department 4%. If you did the same in the palliative area, that is, where you take care of people in the last part of their lives, then you have to put an additional 2%. In other areas. So if you really want to give the regions an opportunity to act as management here and to assess where there is a productivity gain, then you should not have a centrally set rigid target. That is why I understand when the Social Democrats, who are presidents around the regions, are dissatisfied with the declaration of distrust that is being sent in here from Christiansborg. I would like to conclude by thanking the parties which support the proposal • Danish People's Party, Radicals, Alternative and SF. I think we have come a long way and I think we should do everything we can to keep this theme on the agenda and at least back up Danske Regions in succeeding in getting through this in the economic negotiations that are going on right now.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517182913	Thank you, and thank you to the SF for the motion for a resolution on granting citizens with type 1 diabetes the right to treatment with glucose sensor and insulin pump. I would say that I understand the purpose of the proposal and I think that the idea behind the proposal is to ensure that citizens with type 1 diabetes have access to the best possible treatment, and I do not, in fact, disagree with the SF in that respect. I believe that we have all followed the cases where citizens are caught up in a fight between regions and municipalities, in relation to who is the granting authority in this area. I think everyone has thought that, as regards the decision of principle by the Ankestyrelsen, which was expected to be clarified before New Year, but which was then postponed only a little more, and which is now expected by the end of May, it has been frustrating to see that there was no clarity. So I understand the intention behind and aimed at the proposal. I think we've all heard these stories about parents who have to wake their children in the middle of the night to make sure that their blood sugar doesn't gallop in either direction and where technological aids could make not only the lives of the children but for the whole family better. And there is no doubt that here and now there is a problem with the municipalities and regions that have not been able to agree on the responsibility for granting glucose sensors, and this is an issue which I hope will soon be clarified by the Ankestyrelsen and which they have announced they will clarify by the end of May. It is just not always the case that all good wishes we have to ensure better treatment must be met or that all problems must be resolved by inventing a new rule or by putting forward a resolution or a legislative proposal in this Chamber. We need to be careful about rules that risk disregarding professional judgements of what is the best solution in the specific cases. There is no doubt that there are patients with type 1 diabetes who with treatment would have much, much benefit from it, and as already today we can see that in some municipalities and regions too, we benefit from glucose sensors and insulin pumps. But it is not necessarily the case that all patients with type 1 diabetes would benefit from it, and that is where I think it is important that we also allow it to be based precisely on a professional assessment by those doctors who are in contact with our diabetics, what it is for treatment to be initiated. The treatment of diabetes and the whole technology that we are discussing here in relation to glucose sensors and insulin pumps is something that has been constantly evolving. The tools to which the SF's proposal seeks to guarantee the right of citizens, just as we have seen developments overtake the old types of treatment tools, risk being overtaken by new and smarter forms of treatment. That is why I think we should ensure that it is precisely the professional judgement that is the basis for which tools of treatment are used and not what technology we think is the right one at any given time, because then we risk that we in the Folketing may end up doing more harm than good, because we cannot necessarily always keep up with developments as quickly as it goes, and because we cannot minutely come up with new rules every time new knowledge becomes available or every time technology in this field develops. That is why I believe that decisions must be taken on the basis of, among other things. The general guidelines and guidelines of national authorities are taken locally close to the individual citizen and those who have the professional insight and who are responsible for the treatment of the individual, whether they are children, where I think it is a larger target group. from some of the experiences in the Capital Region see that can benefit from these treatment tools, or it is other diabetics in the Type 1 Diabetes Group. As I have said, I am fully aware of the fact that there are a number of pending cases in this area, cases which have often arisen as a result of disagreements between the authorities on the liability of appropriations, and a number of questions have also been answered to the Danish Parliament precisely in connection with this so-called delimitation circular, which clearly states that it is not the citizen who has to be caught up, because then the authorities will have to grant it, and then the fight between the municipality and the region can then be dealt with. It is the municipalities that are responsible for the funding under social legislation if the glucose sensors are to be used as an aid for permanent impairment of function, and it is the regions that have to grant the glucose sensors if it is a treatment tool that is part of a hospital treatment. I look forward to the Ankestyrelsen getting the cases settled so that we can have clarity about the liability of appropriations, and so that we can also help to ensure that the citizens who have waited for too long can see that their cases are getting out of hand. It is, of course, important and also essential that, as a citizen with a disease or disability, you can be sure that you will have the necessary aids and tools, even in good time, as the proposal also indicates with this desire for one month from the moment you are put in perspective until you get it. However, I also think it is important that, when we talk about the total area of diabetes, we relate to the fact that we have jointly put money in the rate pool to make a diabetes action plan and that we must be careful not to make knobfire in this area when we have just agreed together that we need an updated action plan. It is therefore the Government's wish that we discuss these things together in a diabetes action plan and that we should be careful not to think that we can regulate in detail and thus override the professional estimates of which group of diabetics may need which types of treatment tools. I hope that the diabetes action plan will help to ensure that the individual citizen is given more targeted and clear treatment in this area, and that is also the reason why we cannot support the proposal, namely that there is no funding, that we believe that we are in danger of doing more harm than good, that we will wait for the board of directors and that we believe that this should naturally be part of an overall context of the diabetes action plan.

Particularly in the case of children, it is untenable that regions and municipalities are unable to cooperate. I am the mother of a child with a chronic illness, and I know how hard it must be for parents with a child who has diabetes, for example. to have to go up at night and measure blood sugar, especially in view of the fact that there is in fact a technical solution that would facilitate the everyday life of both adults and children. At the moment, a large number of cases are being dealt with by the Ankestyrelsen, namely 350, which deals with the refusal of the municipalities to grant glycose sensors. I hope that when the decisions in principle by the Ankestyrelsen are made, and hopefully they will soon, we will all know who is responsible for the granting of funds. In addition, under the auspices of the Committee on the Close and Consistent Health Care, there are discussions which also address the problem, namely how we ensure that no one is lost between two chairs. And before the summer vacation, there's a report from that edge. So there are several initiatives in progress or on the way which address the problem in one way or another. For us in the Left, it is crucial that the individual patient should be considered individually in the health care system and that he should address both individual issues and individual treatment options. People are different, and even if you have the same diagnosis, there can also be different needs that each one has. This is also true for type 1 diabetic patients. Therefore, it makes good sense that it is an individual assessment to look at who can benefit from a glycose sensor. New developments are constantly taking place, both in relation to the treatment of diabetes and equipment, and it is inappropriate to guarantee the right to equipment that is obsolete. I mentioned earlier our common goal of a new diabetes action plan. I expect the question of glycosesonors and insulin pumps to be an important element and issue which we must address together and clarify during the negotiations. The new diabetes action plan has been financed by 65 million. kr. via the rate pool. The proposal here from the SF does not specify funding. The Left cannot support the motion for a resolution, among other things. Because it's not funded. However, we are happy to contribute to a joint report in this area, based on the fact that regions and municipalities should be able to jointly ensure that citizens are offered the appropriate treatment tools, and the authorities must be able to figure out how to clarify the payment obligations between themselves, and citizens with type 1 diabetes must have access to the same interventions and treatment options, regardless of where they live in the country.

The Danish Parliament acknowledges that the change in the process of preparing the Danish Health Authority's guidance on the investigation and treatment of trannesses has ensured an open and inclusive process in which trans-political organisations and actors from Denmark and abroad have been consulted and involved. The Danish Parliament invites the regions to consider how to organise the analysis and treatment of trannised people, so that future treatment offers for trannised people are actually moved from psychiatry in accordance with the movement of the diagnostic code from 1 January 2017. The Danish Parliament also calls on the government to ensure that, within the framework of the law in force, the same opportunities as other citizens are guaranteed and that they are treated with dignity and respect in the meeting with the health service. • V 109).

So I think it is unbelievable with these accusations that it is only on the pretext that we are trying to say, like politically, that we want more, we want to be better at involving ourselves than others have been. I do not think that this matches the course we have otherwise taken, where we have worked seriously on this in cooperation. Then there's the whole question of this multi-professional and multidisciplinary team, that is, the professional team around it: Which competencies can be relevant? I think it is extremely relevant, as is the case in many other areas of health care, that consideration is being given by the Danish Health Authority to what competences are to be included in such teams, and then it will be organised individually in relation to what the individual person needs; what characterises the course he is going through; what kind of treatment he is going through; what kind of treatment he is going to have to go through; after all, some just want hormones, but not actually castration and various other things; others have some other wishes. It is relevant to take account of individualisation and not the psychic analysis that has been there so far, which has been highly criticised. This does not mean that you are mentally ill or somatically ill, but there may well be professional competences in both fields, which is essentially part of the course of treatment that a transperson should have in the Danish healthcare system. So basically, I am rather sorry that we are taking this debate, but hopefully it will lead us to clarify some of the misunderstandings that may exist. In other words, I have no expectation that everyone will be completely satisfied. I also said in my speech that there will always be some who will pay more attention to professional considerations than to other considerations, and some others who will pay more attention to some other considerations than professional ones. That is how it is in all legislative processes. I hope, however, that the process that has taken place, in which everyone has also had the opportunity to speak, will enable us to come as far as possible in relation to the creation of a new guide which ensures both the respectful and proper involvement of the parties in the field and the transpersonals, but which also takes into account the complex professionalism that may be needed to be present in these processes. Then I really only hope that the cooperation that has been so far in the Folketing can continue in this area, because, as many of the rapporteurs have said, we have come a long way, but there are still areas where there is a way to go in order to ensure proper treatment in this area.

ParlaMint-DK_20170517202908	Thank you to Mrs Kirsten Normann Andersen. Then it is the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Mrs Stine Brix, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20170517202916	In practice, we have actually tried to bring together two debates which should not otherwise be put together, and I think that is quite fine. I do not think it is quite right, as the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party says, that the question and the motion for a resolution deal with the same thing, because I do not think the question as it is put does. But we have actually discussed treatment more widely in the course of the question, and that is why it is quite fine that we have done what we have done here. I think it is important to remember, when we are discussing this matter in the Folketing, how the process has been in some of the other cases where progress has been made for transversals. It was not the Danish Health Service which took the initiative to remove transsexuality from the list of psychiatric diagnoses. It was not the Folketing that took the initiative either. For many, many years, however, it was a demand from both transpersonal organisations and human rights organisations that we should do something. Fortunately, we did, and we finally managed to get the Danish Health Service to do so. It was the same course around the legal change of sex. I also think that we are seeing the same course of action here on the treatment. It is to a large extent those who, for many, many years, are fighting as activists and through the organisations that make us look at this area at all. As Mrs May-Britt Kattrup said earlier in the debate, because there are talented people who address these issues, there are many of us who relate to them at all, otherwise perhaps it was not exactly what we would have seen as the first. The purpose of this motion for a resolution, which is now a few months old, is to put it on the agenda, that we want to see a fundamentally different approach to it, or a different treatment paradigm that I have written in the motion for a resolution, in order to provide a new guide to the treatment of transgenders. What is most important to me is that we really take the consequence of the understanding that I think there is in general international about what transsexuality means, that is to say what it means to have a gender identity which is different from that which one is born with. In fact, there is generally an international recognition that it is only one person who can decide what one's gender identity is. It will never be a doctor or a psychiatrist or, for that matter, anyone else who will be able to decide whether or not you as a human being are transgender; whether you as a human being have a gender identity different from that of the biological sex with which you were born. That recognition does not reflect our treatment practice today, and I do not really believe that this recognition has been incorporated in the draft guidance we have seen now. For the whole mindset is still built on the fact that you have to go through an analysis by a multi-disciplinary team, so far it doesn't matter who has to make an analysis and that you have to live up to an indication, and that you have to have some degree of gender dysphoria, as it says in the guidance. What we want the approach to be in health care is to recognise that it is the individual who knows whether he or she is transgender and needs treatment. That is why we write that the treatment should be based on informed consent, that is to say that you get good knowledge of the benefits and side effects of a treatment, but that you ultimately make a decision on whether you want, for example, hormone treatment or surgery. Therefore, I do not believe that the draft guidance we have seen so far is optimal yet. It goes without saying that this is what the Alternative's rapporteur called for, namely that we should somehow return to the practice that we have had in the past for decades, namely that we should be able to go to a private gynecologist without the need for a psychiatrist and have hormone treatment. It is clear that it is implicit in that approach, and it is also clear from the comments on the resolution that it must of course be an option again. This is also an answer to one of the rapporteurs in the past, whom I do not remember who was who said it should be with a multi-disciplinary team in all contexts. We do not think that there is necessarily to be. It's different if it's necessary. Then I have also taken note of the LGBT Denmark's response to the resolution. They make some good additions which we have not been aware of in the first place. They highlight, among other things, the importance of the European Union's relations with the countries of Central and Eastern Europe and the countries of Central and Eastern Europe and the countries of Central and Eastern Europe. the fact that we in Denmark have a rather poor quality in the case of lower surgery, and they refer to the fact that in Belgium and the Netherlands, both countries are significantly larger than Denmark, there is cooperation where it is done. I think it would be quite obvious that we should also include it in our guidance. It could be a Nordic collaboration, so we make sure that there is a certain volume, so that the surgeons who are going to perform this surgery can also do it with a high quality. Then I would like to highlight the proposal, which also comes from LGBT Denmark, that you should have an offer of access to psychological assistance. We think that's really, really good. So that is to say a little about what is in mind with our motion for a resolution. It's about setting a direction for the guidance. It's a little bit like we agree on everything. Actually, I don't hear it that way. If we can agree on some of this, some of the most important things, then we would like to discuss a report further. But that has to do with the content of such a report. But let us take it forward in committee.
